(respectively, at all weakly associated prime ideals of
\varpi_{i}
) are discrete valuation rings, then the same is true of the local rings of
A
at minimal (respectively, weakly associated prime ideals) of
\varpi
.

Proof.

The spectrum of
A
, as a scheme, is the disjoint union of
{\rm Spec}(A_{i})
, so for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
coincides with the local ring of some
A_{i}
at a corresponding prime ideal of
A_{i}
. Moreover,
A/(\varpi)_{A}=\prod_{i}A_{i}/(\varpi_{i})_{A}
, so a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
A
is minimal over
\mathfrak{p}
(respectively, is a weakly associated prime of
\varpi
) if and only if it corresponds to a prime ideal of some
A_{i}
which is minimal over
\varpi_{i}
(respectively, a weakly associated prime of
\varpi_{i}
).∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in a ring
A
. If
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov, then every weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
A
is a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
. By hypothesis, the
\varpi
-adic completion
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a valuation ring of rank
1
. By
[
17
]
, Ch. 0, Proposition 6.7.3, the maximal ideal of
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is minimal over
\varpi
. Since
A_{\mathfrak{p}}/\varpi A_{\mathfrak{p}}=\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}/\varpi\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, this means that the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is minimal over
\varpi
. The assertion follows. ∎

Our interest in (strongly)
\varpi
-Shilov rings is motivated by the following property of weakly associated prime ideals.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
. Then, for every
n\geq 0
,

(\varpi^{n})_{A}=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)}\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}(\varpi^{n}A_{\mathfrak{p}})=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)}\widehat{\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}(\varpi^{n}\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}),

where
{\rm wAss}(\varpi)
is the set of weakly associated prime ideals of
\varpi
and where, for every prime
\mathfrak{p}
, we denote by
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\widehat{\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
the canonical maps
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
and
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
, with
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
being the
\varpi
-adic completion of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proof.

The case
n=0
follows from the case
n\geq 1
since for any ring
B
with a non-zero-divisor
\varpi\in B
an element
\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}\in B[\varpi^{-1}]
(with
f\in B
,
n\geq 1
) belongs to
B
if and only if
f
belongs to
(\varpi^{n})_{B}
. Thus, given
f\in A
,
n\geq 1
with
f\not\in(\varpi^{n})_{A}
, we want to prove that there exists some
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)
such that the image of
f
in
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
does not belong to
(\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
(note that

(\varpi^{n})_{\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\cap A_{\mathfrak{p}}=(\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}

for all
n\geq 1
, so it suffices to consider
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
instead of
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
). If
f\not\in(\varpi^{n})_{A}
, then
(\varpi^{n})_{A}:_{A}f
is a proper ideal of
A
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal of
A
minimal over
(\varpi^{n})_{A}:_{A}f
. Then

(\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}:_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}f\subseteq\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}},

so
(\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}:_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}f
is a proper ideal of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. It follows that
f\not\in\varpi^{n}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. By
[
10
]
, Theorem 3, the weakly associated prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\varpi^{n}
is also a weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
. The assertion follows.∎

The above proposition also shows that the strong
\varpi
-Shilov property imposes rather strong algebraic conditions on the ring
A
in question (or, more precisely, on the ring extension
A\subseteq A[\varpi^{-1}]
), as is exemplified by the following corollary.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
A
be a ring which is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov for some non-zero-divisor and non-unit
\varpi
. Then
A
is completely integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
.

Proof.

By the proposition,

A=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)}\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}),

where
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}:A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
are the canonical maps. By the hypothesis that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov, the rings
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)
are valuation rings of rank
1
, so
A
is equal to the subring of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
where the corresponding continuous rank
1
valuations are
\leq 1
. We conclude by applying
[
5
]
, Theorem 1.3.1, that
A
is equal to the closed unit ball for the spectral seminorm
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
on
A[\varpi^{-1}]
derived from the canonical extension of the
\varpi
-adic seminorm on
A
. ∎

Proposition
6.13
should also be compared to the following fact.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
. Suppose that

A=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)}\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}(A_{\mathfrak{p}})

or

A=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)}\widehat{\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}),

where
{\rm Min}(\varpi)
denotes the set of prime ideals of
A
minimal over
\varpi
and where
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\widehat{\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
are as in Proposition
6.13
. Then every weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
A
is minimal over
\varpi
.

Proof.

Note that the two equalities are actually equivalent, since
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]=A_{\mathfrak{p}}
for every
\mathfrak{p}
. Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a weakly associated prime of
\varpi
in
A
and let
f\in A
be such that
\mathfrak{q}
is minimal over
(\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f
. In particular,
\frac{f}{\varpi}\not\in A
. If
\mathfrak{q}
is not minimal over
\varpi
, then
\mathfrak{q}
is not contained in any prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
minimal over
\varpi
, so
\mathfrak{q}A_{\mathfrak{p}}=A_{\mathfrak{p}}
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)
. Since
\mathfrak{q}
is minimal over
(\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f
in
A
, we know that there exists no prime of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
containing
((\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f)A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and strictly contained in
\mathfrak{q}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, for any
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)
. It follows that

(\varpi)_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}:_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}f=\mathfrak{q}A_{\mathfrak{p}}=A_{\mathfrak{p}}

for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)
and, therefore,
\frac{f}{\varpi}\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)
. But by our assumption on
A
this implies
f\in(\varpi)_{A}
, a contradiction. Thus we have shown that every weakly associated prime of
\varpi
in
A
is a minimal prime over
\varpi
. ∎

The above results are already reminiscent of the description of the notion of boundary for a Tate ring
\mathcal{A}
or a Tate Huber pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+})
which we gave in Section 3. However, before we describe the Shilov boundaries of Tate rings in this fashion, let us verify that strongly
\varpi
-Shilov rings are actually very common in commutative algebra. This is ensured by the following theorem, which is the first main result of this section.

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of integral domains, where
A
is Noetherian and where
A
and
B
have the same field of fractions. Let
\varpi
be a non-zero non-unit in
A
and in
B
such that
B
is integrally closed in
B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov. More precisely, for every weakly associated prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\varpi
in
B
the separated quotient
B_{\mathfrak{p}}/\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a DVR with uniformizer
\varpi
.

If
B
is moreover Noetherian, then the local rings
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
, for
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm wAss}(\varpi)
, are themselves DVRs and thus
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Krull.

We first prove the following elementary lemma, which will also become important for us later.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension and let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in
A
and in
B
. Suppose that either
A
and
B
are domains or the map
A\hookrightarrow B
is flat. For every minimal prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
over
\varpi
in
B
, the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}\cap A
is minimal over
\varpi
in
A
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{q}B_{\mathfrak{q}}
is minimal over
\varpi
in
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
, it is also minimal over
\overline{(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}}}
(integral closure). If
A
,
B
are domains, the integral ring map
A_{\mathfrak{p}}\to B_{\mathfrak{q}}
is clearly injective, while, if
A\hookrightarrow B
is flat, the map
A_{\mathfrak{p}}\to B_{\mathfrak{q}}
is also injective, being faithfully flat. By
[
36
]
, Proposition 1.6.1, applied to the integral ring extension

A_{\mathfrak{p}}\hookrightarrow B_{\mathfrak{q}},

the map

A_{\mathfrak{p}}/\overline{(\varpi)_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to B_{\mathfrak{q}}/\overline{(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}}}

is injective and thus an integral ring extension. We deduce from this that

\dim(A_{\mathfrak{p}}/(\varpi)_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})=\dim(A_{\mathfrak{p}}/\overline{(\varpi)_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}})=\dim(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/\overline{(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}}})=0,

so
\mathfrak{p}
is minimal over
\varpi
, as desired.∎

Proof of Theorem
6.16
.

The second assertion follows from the first by Krull’s intersection theorem. We first prove the first assertion for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
which is minimal over
\varpi
(so, in particular,
B
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov). Note that
B
is a subextension of the normalization
\overline{A}
of
A
in its field of fractions. For a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
minimal over
\varpi
, choose a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
of
\overline{A}
lying over
\mathfrak{p}
. If there exists a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}_{0}
contained in
\mathfrak{q}
and containing
\varpi
, then
\mathfrak{q}_{0}
lies over
\mathfrak{p}
, by the minimality of
\mathfrak{p}
over
\varpi
, and then necessarily
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}
. Thus
\mathfrak{q}
is minimal over
\varpi
. By the Mori-Nagata Theorem (see, for example,
[
36
]
, Theorem 4.10.5),
\overline{A}
is a Krull domain. This implies that the local ring
\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a DVR with uniformizer
\varpi
, so

\mathfrak{q}\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}=(\varpi)_{\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}}.

Since
B
is integrally closed in
B[\varpi^{-1}]
, the local ring
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is integrally closed in
B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
, so

B_{\mathfrak{p}}/(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{p}}}\to\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}/(\varpi)_{\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}}

is injective by virtue of Proposition
3.12
and
[
36
]
, Proposition 1.6.1. It follows that the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}B_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is generated by
\varpi
. This implies that
B_{\mathfrak{p}}/\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a DVR with uniformizer
\varpi
, as claimed.

It remains to show that every weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
B
is a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
. By Proposition
6.15
, it suffices to prove that

B=(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}_{B}(\varpi)}B_{\mathfrak{p}})\cap B[\varpi^{-1}],

or, equivalently, that

(\varpi)_{B}=(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}_{B}(\varpi)}\varpi B_{\mathfrak{p}})\cap B.

By Proposition
3.12
and
[
36
]
, Proposition 1.6.1,

(\varpi)_{B}=(\varpi)_{\overline{A}}\cap B

and

(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}\cap B}}=(\varpi)_{\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap B

for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
of
\overline{A}
. Since
\overline{A}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov (being a Krull domain), it satisfies

(\varpi)_{\overline{A}}=(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{q}\in{\rm Min}_{\overline{A}}(\varpi)}\varpi\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}})\cap\overline{A},

by Proposition
6.13
. Therefore,

(\varpi)_{B}=(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{q}\in{\rm Min}_{\overline{A}}(\varpi)}\varpi\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{q}})\cap B=(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{q}\in{\rm Min}_{\overline{A}}(\varpi)}\varpi B_{\mathfrak{q}\cap B})\cap B.

But for
\mathfrak{q}
a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
in
\overline{A}
, the intersection
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}\cap B
is a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
in
B
, by Lemma
6.17
.∎

We now relate the weakly and strongly
\varpi
-Shilov conditions to adic spectra (and Berkovich spectra) of the relevant Tate rings. By a generic point of a topological space we mean the generic point of some irreducible component of the space.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in a ring
A
and suppose that
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Consider the specialization map

{\rm sp}:{\rm Spa}(A[\varpi^{-1}],A)\to{\rm Spf}(A),v\mapsto{\rm sp}(v):=\{\,f\in A\mid v(f)<1\,\}.

Then
A
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov if and only if for every generic point
\mathfrak{p}
of
{\rm Spf}(A)
the pre-image
{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\})
consists of a single point. In this case, for every generic point
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spf}(A)
, the map
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
induces a bijection

{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})\tilde{\to}{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\}).

We first establish the following general proposition.

Proposition 6.19
.

Let
A
be a ring and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit such that
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then the pre-image
{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\})
of any
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spf}(A)
is contained in the image of the map

{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})\to{\rm Spa}(A[\varpi^{-1}],A)

induced by

A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}].

Proof.

Let
v
be a continuous valuation on
A[\varpi^{-1}]
with
v(f)\leq 1
for all
f\in A
and with
{\rm sp}(v)=\{\,f\in A\mid v(f)<1\,\}=\mathfrak{p}
. Then
v
extends to a valuation
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
by setting

v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\frac{f}{s})=v(f)

for all
f\in A
,
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
(this is well-defined since
v(s)=1
for all
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
). If
\frac{f}{s}
(
f\in A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
) belongs to
\varpi^{n}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, then

v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\frac{f}{s})=v(f)\leq v(\varpi^{n}),

showing that
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a continuous valuation on the Tate ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
. Thus
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
extends to a continuous valuation on
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
which is bounded above by
1
on
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and whose restriction along the map
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
is
v
.∎

Proof of Proposition
6.18
.

By Lemma
4.28
, it suffices to prove that for any
A
and
\varpi
such that
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
there exists a canonical bijection

{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})\tilde{\to}{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\})

for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
A
minimal over
\varpi
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal of
A
minimal over
\varpi
and consider the map

{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\})\to{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}),v\mapsto v_{\mathfrak{p}},

constructed in the proof of Proposition
6.19
. We claim that the image of the map

{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})\to{\rm Spa}(A[\varpi^{-1}],A),w\mapsto w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]},

where
w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
denotes the restriction along
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
of a continuous valuation
w
on
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
, is
{\rm sp}^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{p}\,\})
and that
w\mapsto w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
is inverse to the map
v\mapsto v_{\mathfrak{p}}
. To prove this, let

w\in{\rm Spa}(A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}],A_{\mathfrak{p}})\simeq{\rm Spa}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}],\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})

and let
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
. Then
w(\frac{s}{1})\leq 1
since
\frac{s}{1}\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and

w(\frac{s}{1})^{-1}=w(\frac{1}{s})\leq 1

since
\frac{1}{s}\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, so
w(\frac{s}{1})=1
. It follows that
w=v_{\mathfrak{p}}
with
v=w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
. Moreover, the center of
w
on
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is contained in
\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and thus the center
{\rm sp}(v)
of
v
on
A
is contained in
\mathfrak{p}
. However, the center of a
\varpi
-adically continuous valuation on
A
is always a prime ideal containing
\varpi
and thus
{\rm sp}(v)=\mathfrak{p}
, by the minimality of
\mathfrak{p}
over
\varpi
. This concludes the proof.∎

We can now similarly characterize strongly
\varpi
-Shilov rings. In what follows, for any Tate Huber pair
(A,A^{+})
, we view the Berkovich spectrum of
\mathcal{M}(A)
, where
A
is endowed with some seminorm defining its topology, as the set of rank
1
points of the adic spectrum
{\rm Spa}(A,A^{+})
.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
and suppose that
A
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
. The set
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm wAss}(\varpi))
of pre-images of weakly associated prime ideals of
\varpi
is always a boundary for
(\mathcal{A},A)
. The ring
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov if and only if
A
is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
(and thus
A=\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
), every generic point
x\in{\rm Spf}(A)
has a unique pre-image under the specialization map

{\rm sp}:{\rm Spa}(\mathcal{A},A)\to{\rm Spf}(A)

and the set

{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})={\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{A}(\varpi))

of pre-images of generic points of
{\rm Spf}(A)
is a boundary for
\mathcal{A}
. In this case,
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary (in the sense of Definition
2.26
).

Proof.

The assertion that
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm wAss}(\varpi))
is always a boundary is a direct consequence of Theorem
3.21
and Proposition
6.13
. Assume that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov. In view of Proposition
6.18
and Corollary
6.14
, we only need to show that
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is the Shilov boundary for
\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, Proposition
6.18
, together with Lemma
4.28
, implies that the pre-image of any
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}}
is a rank
1
point. Therefore,
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is a subset of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
and the question whether it is equal to the Shilov boundary of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
is well-posed. Since, by Lemma
6.12
, we know that every weakly associated prime of
\varpi
is minimal over
\varpi
, the set
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is clearly a boundary. Then the closure
\mathcal{S}
of
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
is also a boundary. It remains to prove that
\mathcal{S}
is the Shilov boundary for
\mathcal{A}
. By
[
21
]
, Ch. 1, Prop. 4, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{S}
is a minimal closed boundary, i.e., that it does not properly contain any smaller closed boundary for
\mathcal{A}
.

By way of contradiction, assume that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is a closed boundary for
\mathcal{A}
which is properly contained in
\mathcal{S}
. If
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
contains
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
, then the fact that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is closed implies
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
, a contradiction. Hence there exists some
v\in{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
which does not belong to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Let

U=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

an open neighbourhood of
v
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
. We prove that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is not a boundary by proving the existence of an element
f\in A
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
and

\{\,w\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})\mid w(f)=1\,\}\subseteq U.

By Proposition
6.18
, we know that

\{v\}={\rm sp}^{-1}(\{{\rm sp}(v)\}),

so

\displaystyle U\supseteq\{v\}={\rm sp}^{-1}(\bigcap_{f\in A\setminus{\rm sp}(v)}D(f))=\bigcap_{f\in A\setminus{\rm sp}(v)}{\rm sp}^{-1}(D(f))

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{f\in A\setminus{\rm sp}(v)}\{\,w\in\mathcal{M}(A[\varpi^{-1}])\mid w(f)=1\,\}.

By compactness of the Berkovich spectrum (
[
5
]
, Theorem 1.2.1) and the fact that

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\{\,w\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})\mid w(f_{i})=1\,\}=\{\,w\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})\mid w(f_{1}\cdot\penalty\ \dots\penalty\ \cdot f_{n})=1\,\}

for any finite family of elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in A
, we can then find some
f\in A\setminus{\rm sp}(v)
with
U\supseteq\{\,w\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})\mid w(f)=1\,\}
. But, since
{\rm sp}(v)
is a prime ideal containing
\varpi
, the property
f\in A\setminus{\rm sp}(v)
implies
f\in A\setminus\sqrt{(\varpi)_{A}}
, so
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
. Thus we have proved that
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is a boundary and its closure is the Shilov boundary for
\mathcal{A}
, assuming that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov.

Conversely, suppose that
A
is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
, every generic point of
{\rm Spf}(A)
has a unique pre-image under the map
{\rm sp}
and that
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is a boundary for
\mathcal{A}
. We want to prove that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov. By Proposition
6.18
, the assumption that every generic point of
{\rm Spf}(A)
has a unique pre-image is equivalent to
A
being weakly
\varpi
-Shilov. That is, for every minimal prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
over
\varpi
the
\varpi
-adically completed local ring
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a valuation ring of rank
1
. It remains to prove that every weakly associated prime of
\varpi
is minimal over
\varpi
. By Proposition
6.15
, it suffices to show that

A=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Min}(\varpi)}\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}(\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}),

where
{\rm Min}(\varpi)
denotes the set of prime ideals of
A
minimal over
\varpi
and
\widehat{\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
denotes the canonical maps
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}[\varpi^{-1}]
. Since
A
is completely integrally closed,

A=\mathcal{A}_{|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1}.

Hence the desired assertion follows from the assumption that
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
is a boundary and Theorem
3.15
.
∎

For algebras over the valuation ring of a sufficiently large nonarchimedean field, the weak and strong
\varpi
-Shilov conditions coincide. To formulate this result, we recall that for a seminormed ring
(\mathcal{A},\lVert\cdot\rVert)
we denote by
(\mathcal{A},\lVert\cdot\rVert)^{\times,m}
the group of units in
A
which are multiplicative with respect to the seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. We simply write
\mathcal{A}^{\times,m}
instead of
(\mathcal{A},\lVert\cdot\rVert)^{\times,m}
if the seminorm is understood from the context.

Proposition 6.21
.

Let
\varpi\in A
be a non-unit and non-zero-divisor in a ring
A
such that
A
is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Suppose that

|\mathcal{A}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\subseteq\sqrt{|\mathcal{A}^{\times,m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}}\cup\{0\}.

Then
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov if and only if it is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov.

Proof.

Suppose that
A
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov. By Theorem
6.20
, we have to prove that the set
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
of pre-images of generic points under the specialization map
{\rm sp}:{\rm Spa}(\mathcal{A},A)\to{\rm Spf}(A)
is a boundary. That is, we have to prove that every
f\in\mathcal{A}
attains its maximum
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
on
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
. Since
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
is power-multiplicative, this is equivalent to proving that for every
f\in\mathcal{A}
there exists an integer
n>0
such that
f^{n}
attains its maximum
|f^{n}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{n}
on
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
. By hypothesis, for every
f\in\mathcal{A}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\neq 0
we can find a seminorm-multiplicative (with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
) unit
c\in\mathcal{A}
such that
|cf^{n}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
for some integer
n>0
. Since
c
is seminorm-multiplicative with respect to the spectral seminorm
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
, we have
|c^{-1}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=|c|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{-1}
, so we obtain
v(c)^{-1}=v(c^{-1})\leq|c|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{-1}
and thus
v(c)=|c|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
for all
v\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
. Therefore, it suffices to prove that every
f\in\mathcal{A}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
attains its maximum on
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(A)_{{\rm gen}})
. But for any such
f
there exists some prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
A
minimal over
\varpi
such that
f\not\in\mathfrak{p}
. The assertion follows from this by definition of the specialization map
{\rm sp}
.∎

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
K
be a nonarchimedean field with pseudo-uniformizer
\varpi
and let
A
be a
\varpi
-torsion-free
K^{\circ}
-algebra such that
A
is a completely integrally closed proper subring of
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Suppose that

|\mathcal{A}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\subseteq\sqrt{|K|}.

Then
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov if and only if it is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov.

In particular, if
K
is such that
|K^{\times}|=\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then the subring of power-bounded elements
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
of any uniform normed
K
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov if and only if it is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov.

In the same vein, we record the following result on uniform Tate rings
\mathcal{A}
whose reduction
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}/\mathcal{A}^{\circ\circ}
is an integral domain.

Proposition 6.23
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a uniform Tate ring with topologically nilpotent unit
\varpi
. Suppose that

|\mathcal{A}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\subseteq\sqrt{|\mathcal{A}^{\times,m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}}\cup\{0\}.

If
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
has a unique minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
(or, equivalently, if
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}/\mathcal{A}^{\circ\circ}
is an integral domain), then
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
is multiplicative and is the unique pre-image under
{\rm sp}
of the unique minimal prime ideal of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. In particular,
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary in the strong sense.

Proof.

We follow the first part of the proof of
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.4.4(ii). Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the unique minimal prime ideal of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Then
\mathfrak{p}=\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}}=\mathcal{A}^{\circ\circ}
, so
\mathfrak{p}
is the open unit ball for the spectral seminorm
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. Let
v\in{\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})
. By the definition of
{\rm sp}
, we know that
v(f)=|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
for all
f\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
with
f\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\setminus\mathfrak{p}
, i.e.,
v(f)=|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
for all
f\in\mathcal{A}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
. But by the assumption on the values of
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
, this implies
v(f)=|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
for all
f\in\mathcal{A}
; in particular,
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=v
is multiplicative and is the only point in
{\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})
. ∎

7
The
\varpi
-Shilov property and coherence

To give additional sufficient conditions for a ring to be
\varpi
-Shilov, we leverage the formalism of
\varpi
-
\ast
-operations introduced in Section 5. The following definition is an analog in this context of the notion of a Prüfer v-multiplication domain (PvMD).

Definition 7.1

(
\ast
-multiplication ring)
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
. Let
\ast:J_{\varpi}(A)\to J_{\varpi}(A)
be a strict
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation and let
\ast_{f}
be the finite-type companion of
\ast
. The ring
A
is called a
\ast
-multiplication ring if for every
f\in A
and every integer
n\geq 1
there exists a finitely generated
A
-submodule
J
of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
with
J\circ_{\ast}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}=A
(equivalently, with
J\circ_{\ast_{f}}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}=A
).

By analogy with the work of Houston, Malik and Mott
[
25
]
on
\ast
-multiplication domains (for a usual
\ast
-operation on the domain), we characterize the notion of
\ast
-multiplication rings for a
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation
\ast
in a number of ways. Hereby, the notion of
\varpi
-valuative rings introduced by Fujiwara-Kato in
[
17
]
and studied in Section 4 of the present paper takes on the role played in the classical case by valuation rings.

For a ring
A
and an ideal
P
of the polynomial ring
A[X]
we write
c(P)\subseteq A
for the content of
P
, the ideal of coefficients of elements of
P
. If
P
is generated by a single polynomial
F\in A[X]
, then
c(P)
is the ideal of
A
generated by the coefficients of
F
and we write
c(F)
instead of
c(P)
.

Theorem 7.2

(Analog of
[
25
]
, Theorem 1.1)
.

Let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zeo-divisor and non-unit in a ring
A
. Let
\ast
be a strict
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation on
A
and let
\ast_{f}
be its companion of finite type. The following are equivalent:

(1)

A
is a
\ast
-multiplication ring.

(2)

A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
and, for every
f\in A
,
n\geq 1
, the ideal

(\varpi^{n}X-f)A[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap A[X]

of the polynomial ring
A[X]
contains an element
F
with
c(F)^{\ast}=A
.

(3)

A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
\varpi
-valuative ring for every maximal
\ast_{f}
-ideal
\mathfrak{p}
.

(4)

A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
\varpi
-valuative ring for every prime
\ast_{f}
-ideal
\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(4): Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime
\ast_{f}
-ideal of
A
. By virtue of Proposition
4.16
(3) and Proposition
4.23
, to prove that
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
\varpi
-valuative ring, it suffices to let
f\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
f\neq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and prove that the ideal
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is invertible. For this it suffices to assume that
f\in A
. Since
A
is a
\ast
-multiplication ring, there exists a finitely generated
A
-submodule
J
of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
with
((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J)^{\ast}=A
. Since
J
is finitely generated, this is equivalent to
((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J)^{\ast_{f}}=A
. Since
\mathfrak{p}
is a
\ast_{f}
-ideal, this implies

(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J\not\subseteq\mathfrak{p}

and thus
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}J_{\mathfrak{p}}=A_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(3): Trivial in view of Lemma
5.23
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2): By Proposition
4.20
, (3) implies that
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a Prüfer subring of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
. By
[
31
]
, Ch. 1, Theorem 5.2(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(4), this means in particular that
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is integrally closed in
A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
, so, by Proposition
5.26
,
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Given
f\in A
,
f\neq 0
,
n\geq 1
, set

P=(\varpi^{n}X-f)A[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap A[X].

By
[
31
]
, Theorem 5.2(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(6), for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
there exists a polynomial
F_{\mathfrak{p}}\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}[X]
with at least one unit coefficient such that
F_{\mathfrak{p}}(\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}})=0
. For every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
, set

B_{\mathfrak{p}}=A_{\mathfrak{p}}/\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\varpi^{k}A_{\mathfrak{p}}.

The image of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
in
B_{\mathfrak{p}}[X]
is a polynomial with coefficients in
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
which has a zero at (the image of)
\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}
and has at least one unit coefficient; in particular, this polynomial does not belong to
(\mathfrak{p}B_{\mathfrak{p}})[X]
.

On the other hand, by Proposition
4.23
, for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
, the
\varpi
-adically separated local ring
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a valuation ring and then, by
[
17
]
, Ch. 0, Proposition 6.7.2,
B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
is a field. Thus, for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
, the ideal of
B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]
consisting of polynomials with a zero at
\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}
is equal to
(\varpi^{n}X-f)B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]
. It follows that

(\varpi^{n}X-f)B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap B_{\mathfrak{p}}[X]\not\subseteq(\mathfrak{p}B_{\mathfrak{p}})[X].

In other words,

1\in(\varpi^{n}X-f)A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]+(\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\varpi^{k}A_{\mathfrak{p}})[X].

This means that
(\varpi^{n}X-f)A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap A_{\mathfrak{p}}[X]
contains a polynomial whose constant coefficient lies in
1-\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\varpi^{k}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
; in particular, the constant coefficient of this polynomial is a unit in
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. In particular,

(\varpi^{n}X-f)A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap A_{\mathfrak{p}}[X]\not\subseteq(\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}})[X]

for every
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
. Therefore, for each
\mathfrak{p}\in{\rm Max}_{\ast_{f}}(A)
we have
c(P)\not\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
. Consequently,
c(P)^{\ast_{f}}=A
. Since
\ast_{f}
is the finite-type companion of
\ast
, there exists a finitely generated subideal
J
of
c(P)
with
1\in J^{\ast}
, so that
J^{\ast}=A
. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}\in P
be polynomials whose coefficients generate
J
. One can then construct a polynomial
F\in(G_{1},\dots,G_{n})_{A[X]}
with
c(F)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(G_{i})
, so that
c(F)^{\ast}=A
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Let
f\in A
,
n\geq 1
, and choose
F\in(\varpi^{n}X-f)A[\varpi^{-1}][X]\cap A[X]
with
c(F)^{\ast}=A
. Write

F=(\varpi^{n}X-f)H

for some
H\in A[\varpi^{-1}][X]
. Then
A=c(F)^{\ast}\subseteq(c(\varpi^{n}X-f)c(H))^{\ast}=((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}c(H))^{\ast}
. Hence it remains to show that
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}c(H)\subseteq A
. By the Dedekind-Mertens Lemma,

(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}c(H)(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}^{m}=c((\varpi^{n}X-f)H)(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}^{m}\subseteq(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}^{m},

where
m
is the degree of the polynomial
H\in A[\varpi^{-1}][X]
. By the assumption that
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
and
[
36
]
, Lemma 2.1.8, this implies that
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}c(H)\subseteq A
, as desired.∎

The importance of the notion of
\ast
-multiplication rings for our purposes arises from the following theorem.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in a reduced ring
A
and let
\ast
be a strict
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation on
J_{\varpi}(A)
. If
A
is a
\ast
-multiplication ring, then
A
is a
\varpi
-Shilov ring.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
A
. Choose
f\in A
with
\mathfrak{p}
minimal over
(\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f=A:_{A}\frac{f}{\varpi}
. By Lemma
5.2
,
(\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f
is a
\varpi
-v-ideal (
\varpi
-divisorial ideal). Since by Proposition
5.18
the
\varpi
-v-operation is the coarsest strict
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation,
(\varpi)_{A}:_{A}f
is also a
\ast
-ideal. By Proposition
5.25
, it follows that
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime
\ast_{f}
-ideal. We conclude by Theorem
7.2
(1)
\Rightarrow
(4) (in conjunction with Corollary
4.24
) that
\widehat{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a valuation ring. ∎

To find more practical sufficient criteria for being a
\varpi
-Shilov ring, we use the following analogs of the notions of a finite conductor ring (FC ring) and of a v-coherent ring.

Definition 7.4

(
\varpi
-FC ring)
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in a ring
A
. We say that
A
is a
\varpi
-(finite conductor) ring, or
\varpi
-FC ring, if for every
f\in A
and every integer
n\geq 1
the
\varpi
-residual
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
is finitely generated as an
A
-submodule of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
.

Definition 7.5

(
\ast
-coherent ring)
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
and let
\ast:J_{\varpi}(A)\to J_{\varpi}(A)
be a strict
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation. We say that the ring
A
is
\ast
-coherent if for every
f\in A
and every integer
n\geq 1
the
\varpi
-residual
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
is
\ast
-finite, i.e., there exits a finitely generated
A
-submodule
J
of
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
such that
J^{\ast}=A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
.

In particular,
A
is called
\varpi
-v-coherent if for every
f\in A
and every integer
n\geq 1
the
A
-submodule
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
is equal to
J_{v}=A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}J)
for some finitely generated
A
-submodule
J
of
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
.

It is clear that a
\varpi
-FC ring is
\ast
-coherent for every
\varpi
-
\ast
-operation
\ast
. On the other hand, the name ’
\varpi
-(finite conductor) ring’ is justified by the following lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
A
be a ring such that
{\rm Ann}(f)
is a finitely generated ideal of
A
for every
f\in A
and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in
A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

A
is a
\varpi
-FC ring.

(2)

For every
f\in A
and
n\geq 1
the conductor
A:_{A}\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}=(\varpi^{n})_{A}:_{A}f
of
\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}\in A[\varpi^{-1}]
in
A
is a finitely generated ideal of
A
.

(3)

For every
f\in A
and
n\geq 1
the intersection
(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A}
is finitely generated.

Proof.

Follows from the two short exact sequences
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0 }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Ann}(f)[\varpi^{-1}]={\rm Ann}(\varpi^{n}f)[\varpi^{-1}] }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A} }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A} }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Ann}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A:_{A}\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}} }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A} }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

where the map
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}\to(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A}
is multiplication by
\varpi^{n}f
and the map
A:_{A}\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}\to(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A}
is multiplication by
f
(to show that the two maps are surjective, choose
g\in(f)_{A}\cap(\varpi^{n})_{A}
and write it as
g=hf=h^{\prime}\varpi^{n}
for some
h,h^{\prime}\in A
; then
g=\frac{h}{\varpi^{n}}\varpi^{n}f
and

\frac{h}{\varpi^{n}}\in A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}

as well as
h\in A:_{A}\frac{f}{\varpi^{n}}
).∎

Corollary 7.7
.

Every finite conductor ring
A
(in the sense of
[
19
]
) and, in particular, every coherent ring
A
, is a
\varpi
-FC ring, for every non-zero-divisor and non-unit
\varpi\in A
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
A
be a
\varpi
-FC ring, for some non-zero-divisor and non-unit
\varpi\in A
. If
S\subseteq A
is a multiplicative subset containing
1
and not containing any power of
\varpi
, then the localization
S^{-1}A
is again a
\varpi
-FC ring.

Proof.

By abuse of notation, we identify elements
f\in A
with their images
\frac{f}{1}
in
S^{-1}A
. For
f\in S^{-1}A
and
n\geq 1
, we have to prove that the
S^{-1}A
-submodule

S^{-1}A:_{S^{-1}A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{S^{-1}A}

is finitely generated. It suffices to assume that
f\in A
. In this case, let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
be generators of the
A
-submodule
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
. We prove that (the images of)
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
also generate
S^{-1}A:_{S^{-1}A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{S^{-1}A}
as an
S^{-1}A
-module. Let
g\in A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
t\in S
be such that
\frac{g}{t}f,\frac{g}{t}\varpi^{n}\in S^{-1}A
. Then there exists some
t^{\prime}\in S
with
t^{\prime}gf,t^{\prime}g\varpi^{n}\in A
. Thus
t^{\prime}g\in(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})_{A}
and, consequently,
\frac{g}{t}\in(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})_{S^{-1}A}
. ∎

We obtain the following sufficient criterion for being a
\varpi
-Shilov ring.

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
such that
A
is integrally closed in the Tate ring
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
. If
A
is a
\varpi
-FC ring, then
A
is
\varpi
-Shilov.

Corollary 7.10
.

If
A
is a coherent ring which is integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
for some non-unit non-zero-divisor
\varpi\in A
, then
A
is
\varpi
-Shilov.

Corollary 7.11
.

If
A
is a Noetherian ring which is integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
for some non-unit non-zero-divisor
\varpi\in A
, then
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Krull.

Proof.

Combine Corollary
7.10
and Lemma
6.4
. ∎

Our proof of Theorem
7.9
is inspired by Zafrullah’s proof of Theorem 2 in
[
39
]
and, in particular, by the proof of Lemma 5 in that paper. We split most of the proof into a sequence of lemmas.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
A
be a ring with a non-zero-divisor and non-unit
\varpi\in A
and let
\mathcal{A}^{+}
be the integral closure of
A
in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
. If
I\subseteq A
is a finitely generated
A
-submodule of
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
with
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
finitely generated, then
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{+}
.

Proof.

Note that
f\in A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I)
if and only if

f(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I)\subseteq A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I.

Since
I
is finitely generated, it is bounded, so
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
contains some power of
\varpi
. In particular, the finitely generated
A
-module
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is also a faithful
A[f]
-module,
\varpi
being a non-zero-divisor on
A[f]
. Hence the assertion follows from
[
36
]
, Lemma 2.1.8. ∎

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
A
be a local ring with a non-unit non-zero-divisor
\varpi
. Suppose that
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
and that the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
A
is a
\varpi
-t-ideal. Then a finitely generated ideal
I
of
A
is invertible if and only if
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is finitely generated.

Proof.

If
I
is invertible, then, by Proposition
4.16
(1),
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is its inverse. In particular,
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is an invertible
\varpi
-fractional ideal and then
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is finitely generated by Proposition
4.16
(2). Conversely, suppose that
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I
is finitely generated. By Lemma
7.12
, this implies that
(I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I))_{v}=A
. But this already means that
I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I)=A
, since otherwise
I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I)
is a proper finitely generated ideal of
A
and then
(I(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}I))_{v}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}
by the assumption that
\mathfrak{m}
is a
\varpi
-t-ideal.∎

Proof of Theorem
7.9
.

It suffices to prove that for every weakly associated prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\varpi
in
A
the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is
\varpi
-valuative. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a weakly associated prime of
\varpi
. By Proposition
4.16
(3) and Proposition
4.23
, to prove that
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is
\varpi
-valuative, it suffices to prove that the ideal
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is invertible for every
f\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and every integer
n\geq 1
. Fix some
f\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and some integer
n\geq 1
. Since
A
is integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
, the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is integrally closed in
A_{\mathfrak{p}}[\varpi^{-1}]
. Since
A
is a
\varpi
-FC ring,
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
\varpi
-FC ring, by Lemma
7.8
. By Lemma
5.24
, the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
\varpi
-t-ideal. We can then apply Lemma
7.13
to the finitely generated ideal
(f,\varpi^{n})_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
to conclude.∎

We deduce the following consequence of Theorem
7.9
for Noetherian rings (which also gives another proof of Lemma
6.4
).
We supplement Theorem
7.9
and Theorem
7.3
with the following criterion for being a
\varpi
-v-multiplication ring.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
\varpi
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
. Suppose that
A
is completely integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then
A
is a
\varpi
-v-multiplication ring if and only if
A
is
\varpi
-v-coherent.

Proof.

Suppose that
A
is a
\varpi
-v-multiplication ring. Let
f\in A\setminus\{0\}
and
n\geq 1
and let
J
be a finitely generated
A
-submodule of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
with
((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J)_{v}=A
. By
[
30
]
, Ch. 3, Proposition 4.1a), this means that
((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J_{v})_{v}=A
. By loc. cit., Proposition 4.5a), this means that

J_{v}=A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A},

showing that
A
is
\varpi
-v-coherent.

Conversely, suppose that
A
is
\varpi
-v-coherent. Let
f\in A\setminus\{0\}
and let
n\geq 1
be an integer. By Theorem
5.32
, the hypothesis that
A
be completely integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
implies that

((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}(A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}))_{v}=A.

By the
\varpi
-v-coherence of
A
, there is a finitely generated submodule
J
of
A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
with
J_{v}=A:_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}(f,\varpi^{n})_{A}
. We conclude by means of
[
30
]
, Ch. 3, Prop. 4.1a), that
((f,\varpi^{n})_{A}J)_{v}=A
.∎

We summarize some of our findings in the following theorem.

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a uniform Tate ring with topologically nilpotent unit
\varpi
. Suppose that one of the following is true.

(1)

The subring of power-bounded elements
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is Noetherian.

(2)

The subring of power-bounded elements
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is
\varpi
-v-coherent and

|\mathcal{A}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\subseteq\sqrt{|\mathcal{A}^{\times,m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}}\cup\{0\}.

Then
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov and thus
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary. In case (1),
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary in the strong sense.

Proof.

(1) By Corollary
7.11
,
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov (and even strongly
\varpi
-Krull), so
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary, by Theorem
6.20
. But the Shilov boundary for
\mathcal{A}
is necessarily finite (apply Corollary
3.17
, or use that
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}/(\varpi)_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}
has only finitely many minimal prime ideals). Therefore, every subset of the Shilov boundary is closed and thus
\mathcal{A}
must also satisfy Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary in the strong sense.

(2) It follows from Theorem
7.3
and Theorem
7.14
that
\mathcal{A}
is
\varpi
-Shilov. It then follows from Proposition
6.21
that
A
is also strongly
\varpi
-Shilov under the given assumption on the spectral seminorm.∎

Remark 7.16
.

By the Reduced Fiber Theorem for affinoid algebras (see, for example,
[
33
]
, Theorem 3.4.2), the subring of power-bounded elements
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
of any reduced affinoid algebra
\mathcal{A}
(in the sense of Tate) over a separably closed nonarchimedean field
K
is topologically of finite type over the valuation ring
K^{\circ}
, while, by a result of Fujiwara-Gabber-Kato (
[
16
]
, Corollary 7.3.6), every
K^{\circ}
-algebra topologically of finite type is a coherent ring. Thus, Theorem
7.15
(2) immediately recovers Berkovich’s original result (Proposition
1.7
) for Tate-affinoid algebras over a separably closed nonarchimedean field
K
.

8
Stability under integral extensions

In this section we consider stability of Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary under completed integral extensions. By Theorem
6.20
, this amounts to checking stability under integral extensions for the strong
\varpi
-Shilov property. The first step is studying the analogous question for the weak
\varpi
-Shilov property.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in
A
and in
B
such that
B
is integrally closed in
B[\varpi^{-1}]
.

(1)

If
A
is
\varpi
-valuative, then so is
B
.

(2)

If the
\varpi
-adic completion of
A
is a valuation ring of rank
1
and
B
is a local ring, then the
\varpi
-adic completion of
B
is also a valuation ring of rank
1
.

(3)

If
A
is a
\varpi
-adically separated valuation ring of rank
1
and
B
is a local ring, then
B
is a
\varpi
-adically separated valuation ring of rank
1
.

Proof.

(1) Note that
B
is the integral closure of
A
inside
B[\varpi^{-1}]
. If
A
is
\varpi
-valuative, then so is
B
, by Proposition
4.20
and
[
31
]
, Ch. I, Theorem 5.9.

(2) Suppose that the
\varpi
-adic completion of
A
is a valuation ring of rank
1
and that
B
is a local ring. By (1),
B
is
\varpi
-valuative, so, by Corollary
4.25
, to prove that the
\varpi
-adic completion of
B
is a valuation ring of rank
1
, it suffices to prove that the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
of
B
is a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
. But
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
restricts to the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
of
A
, which is minimal over
\varpi
.

(3) Now assume that
A
is a valuation ring of rank
1
. By (1) and (2) (and in view of Proposition
4.23
), it suffices to prove that
B
is
\varpi
-adically separated. Since
A
is a
\varpi
-adically separated valuation ring of rank
1
, the localization
A[\varpi^{-1}]
is a field (
[
17
]
, Ch. 0, Proposition 6.7.2). Then the integral extension
B[\varpi^{-1}]
of
A[\varpi^{-1}]
is also a field. But in this case the ideal

\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}B=(\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}B)[\varpi^{-1}]

of
B[\varpi^{-1}]
must be zero.∎

Remark 8.2
.

Note that in the above proposition we did not assume that
B
is a normal domain, in which case the statement that
B
is a rank
1
valuation ring whenever
A
is would also follow from the more classical special case of
[
31
]
, Ch. I, Theorem 5.9, saying that the integral closure of a Prüfer domain inside an algebraic extension of its fraction field is a Prüfer domain.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in
A
and in
B
such that
B
is integrally closed in
B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Suppose that either
A
and
B
are domains, or that the map
A\hookrightarrow B
is flat. If
A
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov (respectively, weakly
\varpi
-Krull), then so is
B
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a prime ideal of
B
minimal over
\varpi
.
By Lemma
6.17
,
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}\cap A
is a minimal prime ideal over
\varpi
in
A
. Hence the assertion follows from Proposition
8.1
applied to the integral ring extension
A_{\mathfrak{p}}\hookrightarrow B_{\mathfrak{q}}
.∎

We deduce from Proposition
8.3
another sufficient condition for a Tate ring
\mathcal{A}
to satisfy Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary. Note that this settles cases (5) and (6) of Theorem
1.10
in the introduction.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a uniform Tate ring, with topologically nilpotent unit
\varpi
, and let
\mathcal{B}
be a uniform Tate ring which is an integral extension of
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is an integral extension of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Suppose that the spectral seminorm
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
on
\mathcal{B}
satisfies

|\mathcal{B}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\subseteq\sqrt{|\mathcal{B}^{\times,m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}}\cup\{0\}.

Suppose further that either
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are domains, or the map
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is flat. If
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary, then so does
\mathcal{B}
(and hence so does its completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
).

Proof.

By the hypothesis, Theorem
6.20
and Proposition
6.21
, we only need to prove that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov. But
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is integral over
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is weakly (and even strongly)
\varpi
-Shilov by Theorem
6.20
. We conclude by applying Proposition
8.3
.∎

Next, we prove a very general result on preservation of the strong
\varpi
-Shilov property for Noetherian domains.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
A
be a Noetherian domain with a non-zero non-unit
\varpi
. Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of domains such that
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov. In particular, the
\varpi
-adic completion
\widehat{B}
of
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov. Moreover, if
A
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
, then
B
is also strongly
\varpi
-Krull.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
, let
\mathcal{A}^{+}
be the integral closure of
A
in
\mathcal{A}
. By Theorem
6.16
applied to
\mathcal{A}^{+}
, we see that
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov, and is strongly
\varpi
-Krull if
A=\mathcal{A}^{+}
. We already know (by Proposition
8.3
) that the integral extension
B
of
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov (and weakly
\varpi
-Krull in the case when
A=\mathcal{A}^{+}
), so it remains to prove that every weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
B
is a minimal prime of
\varpi
. To this end, let
f\in B
with
f\not\in(\varpi)_{B}
and consider a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
minimal over
(\varpi)_{B}:_{B}f
. We can find an
A
-subalgebra
C
of
B
finite over
A
such that
f\in C
. Let
\mathcal{C}^{+}
be the integral closure of
C
in
\mathcal{C}=C[\varpi^{-1}]
. Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{+}
be such that
gf\in(\varpi)_{B}
. By Proposition
3.12
and
[
36
]
, Proposition 1.6.1, this implies
gf\in(\varpi)_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}
. Thus we see that

((\varpi)_{B}:_{B}f)\cap\mathcal{C}^{+}=(\varpi)_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}:_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}f,

and analogously if we replace
B
,
\mathcal{C}^{+}
with their local rings at
\mathfrak{q}
and at
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathcal{C}^{+}
. Then

\dim((\mathcal{C}^{+})_{\mathfrak{p}}/((\varpi)_{(\mathcal{C}^{+})_{\mathfrak{p}}}:_{(\mathcal{C}^{+})_{\mathfrak{p}}}f))=\dim(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/((\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}}:_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}}f))=0,

i.e.,
\mathfrak{p}
is minimal over
(\varpi)_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}:_{\mathcal{C}^{+}}f
. By Theorem
6.16
, every weakly associated prime ideal of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{C}^{+}
is minimal over
\varpi
, the domain
\mathcal{C}^{+}
being an integral extension of the Noetherian domain
C
with the same field of fractions. In particular,
\mathfrak{p}
is minimal over
\varpi
. But then
\dim(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/(\varpi)_{B_{\mathfrak{q}}})=\dim(A_{\mathfrak{p}}/(\varpi)_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}})=0
, so
\mathfrak{q}
is minimal over
\varpi
, as desired.∎

As an immediate consequence of Theorem
8.5
, we obtain a result on the completed absolute integral closure of any Noetherian domain.

Corollary 8.6
.

For every Noetherian domain
R
and for every non-zero non-unit
\varpi\in R
, the absolute integral closure
R^{+}
of
R
is strongly
\varpi
-Krull and its
\varpi
-adic completion
\widehat{R^{+}}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov.

Remark 8.7
.

By contrast, as we explained in Example
6.10
, the ring
\widehat{R^{+}}
is not strongly
\varpi
-Krull, by a result of Heitmann and Ma,
[
23
]
, Proposition 4.10.

Of course, Theorem
8.5
implies that completed integral extensions of uniform Tate rings with Noetherian ring of definition satisfy Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary. With some more work, we can deduce from Theorem
8.5
that such completed integral extensions actually satisfy Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary in the strong sense.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a uniform Tate domain, with topologically nilpotent unit
\varpi
, admitting a Noetherian ring of definition containing
\varpi
. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is an integral extension of uniform Tate domains such that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is integral over
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. Then
\mathcal{B}
(and, consequently, also its completion
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
) satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary in the strong sense.

We will deduce Theorem
8.8
from a series of lemmas.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension, let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and non-unit in
A
and in
B
and let
(C_{i})_{i}
be a direct system of subextensions of
B
with

B=\varinjlim_{i}C_{i}.

Suppose that either
A
,
B
are domains or the maps
C_{i}\hookrightarrow B
are flat. Consider the Tate rings
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
and
\mathcal{C}_{i}=C_{i}[\varpi^{-1}]
. An element
v\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
belongs to
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{B}(\varpi))
if and only if
v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\in{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{C_{i}}(\varpi))
for every
i
(or, equivalently, for some
i
).

Proof.

Suppose that
v\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
is an element of the Berkovich spectrum
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
such that
{\rm sp}(v)
is a minimal prime of
\varpi
in
B
. By Lemma
6.17
,

{\rm sp}(v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}})={\rm sp}(v)\cap C_{i}

is a minimal prime of
\varpi
in
C_{i}
. Conversely, if
{\rm sp}(v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}})={\rm sp}(v)\cap C_{i}
is minimal over
\varpi
in
C_{i}
for some
i
, then
{\rm sp}(v)
itself is minimal over
\varpi
in
B
. ∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an injective ring map and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor in
A
and in
B
. Let
(C_{i})_{i}
be a direct system of
A
-subalgebras of
B
with injective transition maps such that
B=\varinjlim_{i}C_{i}
. Consider the Tate rings
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}=C_{i}[\varpi^{-1}]
, and, for every
i
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
be a closed subset of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
. Then the subset

\mathcal{S}=\{\,v\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})\mid v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\,\}

is a closed subset of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
.

Proof.

Let
(v_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{S}
with limit
v
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
. Thus
v_{n}(f)\to v(f)
for all
f\in\mathcal{B}
. In particular, for every
i
, we have
v_{n}(f)\to v(f)
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
. This means that
v_{n}|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\to v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
. This means that
v|_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
for every
i
, since
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is closed in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
for every
i
. In other words,
v\in\mathcal{S}
. ∎

Lemma 8.11
.

For any reduced Noetherian ring
A
and any non-zero-divisor and non-unit
\varpi\in A
, the integral closure
\mathcal{A}^{+}
of
A
inside
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
has only finitely many minimal prime ideals of
\varpi
.

Proof.

The ring
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is integral over
A
with the same total ring of fractions, so it is contained in the normalization
\overline{A}
of the reduced Noetherian ring
A
in its total ring of fractions. By the Nagata-Mori Theorem (
[
36
]
, Theorem 4.10.5),
\overline{A}
is a finite product of Krull domains, so there exist only finitely many minimal primes of
\varpi
in
\overline{A}
. But any minimal prime of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is the restriction of a minimal prime of
\varpi
in its integral extension
\overline{A}
. ∎

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of integral domains, where
A
is Noetherian, and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero non-unit in
A
and in
B
such that
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{B}(\varpi))
is closed in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
.

Proof.

Write
B
as a direct limit of finite subextensions
C_{i}
of
A
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}=C_{i}[\varpi^{-1}]
and let
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
denote the integral closure of
C_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. By Lemma
8.11
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
has only finitely many minimal prime ideals of
\varpi
. By Theorem
6.16
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov, so, in particular, every minimal prime of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
has a unique pre-image under the specialization map
{\rm sp}:{\rm Spa}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+})\to{\rm Spf}(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+})
. It follows that the set
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}}(\varpi))
is finite and hence a closed subset of the Berkovich spectrum
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
. But

B=\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+},

the ring
B
being integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
, so, by Lemma
8.9
and Lemma
8.10
,
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{B}(\varpi))
is closed in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
8.8
.

By Theorem
8.5
and Theorem
6.20
, we know that
\mathcal{B}
satisfies Berkovich’s description of the Shilov boundary, so every minimal prime of
\varpi
in
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
has a unique pre-image in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
under
{\rm sp}
and the Shilov boundary for
\mathcal{B}
is given by the closure of
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{\mathcal{B}^{\circ}}(\varpi))
. But by Lemma
8.12
, the subset
{\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Min}_{\mathcal{B}^{\circ}}(\varpi))
of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B})
is already closed.∎

Remark 8.13
.

This finishes the proof of case (1) of Theorem
1.10
in the introduction, so now the entirety of Theorem
1.10
is proved.

We now want to remove the Noetherian assumption in Theorem
8.5
, which we are able to do at the cost of assuming that the domain
A
(or, equivalently, the Tate domain
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
) is normal. We begin with some general observations concerning seminorms on integral ring extensions. For a ring
A
and a non-zero-divisor
\varpi\in A
we denote by
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}
the spectral seminorm on
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
derived from the canonical extension
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{A,\varpi}
of the
\varpi
-adic seminorm on
A
(but recall from Lemma
2.21
that this depends only on the topologically nilpotent unit
\varpi\in\mathcal{A}
, not on the choice of ring of definition
A
).

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of rings, let
\varpi\in A
be a non-unit and a non-zero-divisor in both
A
and
B
and suppose that
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then the map
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to B[\varpi^{-1}]
is an isometry when the source and target are endowed with their respective spectral seminorms
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}
and
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},B,\varpi}
.

Proof.

Since
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
B[\varpi^{-1}]
, Proposition
3.12
and
[
36
]
, Proposition 1.6.1, entail that
A\to B
is an isometry for the respective
\varpi
-adic seminorms. This formally implies that
A[\varpi^{-1}]\to B[\varpi^{-1}]
is an isometry for the canonical extensions
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{A,\varpi}
and
\rVert\cdot\rVert_{B,\varpi}
of the
\varpi
-adic seminorms of
A
and
B
. We conclude by Lemma
2.10
. ∎

For an integral extension
A\hookrightarrow B
and
\varpi\in A
as above, the lemma allows us to denote both spectral seminorms
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}
and
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},B,\varpi}
by the same symbol
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor and a non-unit in a ring
A
and let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension. For a multiplicative seminorm
w
on
B[\varpi^{-1}]
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

w
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
;

(2)

the restriction
w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
of
w
to
A[\varpi^{-1}]
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.14
, we only need to prove (2)
\Rightarrow
(1). Suppose that
v=w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. Then
w(\varpi)\leq|\varpi|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=\frac{1}{2}
and
w(\varpi)^{-1}=w(\varpi^{-1})\leq|\varpi^{-1}|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}=2
. Thus, by Lemma
2.20
, it suffices to prove that
w
is continuous with respect to the canonical extension
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{B,\varpi}
of the
\varpi
-adic seminorm on
B
. Thus we have to prove that for every
r>0
the subset
\{\,f\in B\mid w(f)<r\,\}
is open with respect to the topology on
B[\varpi^{-1}]
defined by the pair of definition
(B,\varpi)
. Given
r>0
, choose
n\geq 1
such that
\lVert\varpi^{n}\rVert_{A,\varpi}=2^{-n}<r
. Then
w(\varpi^{n})<r
, so

\varpi^{n}B\subseteq\{\,f\in B\mid w(f)<r\,\},

provided that
w(f)\leq 1
for all
f\in B
.

Hence it remains to prove that
w(f)\leq 1
for all
f\in B
. To this end, let

f^{n}+a_{1}f^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}f+a_{n}=0

be an equation of integral dependence of
f\in B
over
A
. Then

w(f^{n})\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}w(a_{i})w(f^{n-i}).

Choose
i\in\{\,1,\dots,n\,\}
such that
w(f)^{n}=w(f^{n})\leq w(a_{i})w(f^{n-i})=w(a_{i})w(f)^{n-i}
, so that

w(f)\leq w(a_{i})^{1/i}.

Since
w|_{A[\varpi^{-1}]}
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}
, we have
w(a_{i})\leq 1
for all
i
. It follows that
w(f)\leq 1
, as desired. ∎

We deduce from the above lemma the following more general statement.

Lemma 8.16
.

In the situation of the above lemma, the following are equivalent for a power-multiplicative seminorm
|\cdot|
on
B[\varpi^{-1}]
:

(1)

|\cdot|
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
;

(2)

the restriction of
|\cdot|
to
A[\varpi^{-1}]
is bounded with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},A,\varpi}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.10
, a power-multiplicative seminorm
|\cdot|
on a ring is bounded with respect to some seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
if and only if it is bounded above by
\lVert\cdot\rVert
, i.e., if and only if
|\cdot|\leq\lVert\cdot\rVert
. By virtue of
[
5
]
, Theorem 1.3.1, a power-multiplicative seminorm
|\cdot|
on a ring is bounded above by some seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
if and only if every multiplicative seminorm bounded above by
|\cdot|
is bounded with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. Hence the assertion follows from Lemma
8.15
. ∎

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral ring extension and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero-divisor in
A
and in
B
. Suppose that
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Set
U=\mathcal{B}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{A}
and let
U^{\prime}
be the subset of elements of
\mathcal{B}
which satisfy an equation of integral dependence with coefficients in
U
. Let
\mathcal{B}^{+}
be the integral closure of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
inside
\mathcal{B}
. Then
U=\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}}
and
U^{\prime}=\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{B}^{+}}}
.

Proof.

Since
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, we know from Lemma
4.26
that the Tate rings
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
is uniform, so
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
is a seminorm defining the topology on each of them (see Lemma
2.23
and its proof). It follows from this and Lemma
8.14
that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\circ\circ}=\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}}
. Consequently,
U^{\prime}
is the subset of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
consisting of elements satisfying an equation of integral dependence with coefficients in
\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}}
. By
[
11
]
, Lemma 1, this means that
U^{\prime}=\sqrt{(\varpi)_{\mathcal{B}^{+}}}
.∎

Proposition 8.18

(Analog of
[
21
]
, Ch. II, Prop. 2)
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of rings, let
\varpi\in A
be a non-unit and a non-zero-divisor in both
A
and
B
and suppose that
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Then for all
f\in\mathcal{B}
the spectral seminorm
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
of
f
has the form

|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=\inf(\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}),

where the infimum is taken over all equations of integral dependence

X^{n}+a_{1}X^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}X+a_{n}=0,

a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
, satisfied by
f
.

Proof.

Our proof follows the strategy of the proof of
[
21
]
, Ch. II, Proposition 2, using our results on generalized gauges from Section 2 in place of the more classical results on gauges on
K
-vector spaces over a nonarchimedean field
K
used by Guennebaud in loc. cit.

Define a function

\displaystyle\nu:\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle \nu(f)=\inf(\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}),

where the infimum is taken over all equations of integral dependence

f^{n}+a_{1}f^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}f+a_{n}=0

with
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
satisfied by
f
. It is readily seen that
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq\nu
. Indeed, if
f^{n}+a_{1}f^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}f+a_{n}=0
is an equation of integral dependence over
A[\varpi^{-1}]
, we have

|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{n}=|f^{n}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq\max_{0\leq i\leq n-1}|a_{n-i}f^{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq\max_{0\leq i\leq n-1}|a_{n-i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{i},

so there exists some
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{n-i}\leq|a_{n-i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. Since
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is submetric with respect to the two spectral seminorms, it follows that

|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i},

as claimed.

Let
U=\mathcal{B}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\circ\circ}
and let
U^{\prime}
be the set of
f\in\mathcal{B}
which satisfy an equation of integral dependence with coefficients in
U
. In other words,
U^{\prime}=B[\varpi^{-1}]_{\nu<1}
. Then, by Lemma
8.17
,
U^{\prime}
is a radical ideal in the integrally closed open subring
\mathcal{B}^{+}
of
\mathcal{B}
. In particular,
(U^{\prime},\varpi)
is a weak pair of definition of the multiplicative monoid of
\mathcal{B}
, so we can consider the generalized gauge
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
of
(U^{\prime},\varpi)
(see Definition
2.29
). Since
U^{\prime}
is also an integrally closed subset of
\mathcal{B}
, all hypotheses of Proposition
2.37
are satisfied by the subring
\mathcal{B}^{+}
of
\mathcal{B}
and its ideal
U^{\prime}
, and we deduce from that proposition that the generalized gauge
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
of
(U^{\prime},\varpi)
is a ring seminorm on
\mathcal{B}
. In the next step, we prove that the function
\nu
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
2.33
, so that
\nu\leq p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
by that lemma.

To verify that
\nu(\varpi^{m}f)\leq 2^{-m}\nu(f)=|\varpi^{m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\nu(f)
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\in\mathcal{B}
(and hence actually
\nu(\varpi^{m}f)=|\varpi^{m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\nu(f)
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\in\mathcal{B}
), let
f\in\mathcal{B}
,
m\geq 1
and let
f^{n}+a_{1}f^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}f+a_{n}=0
be an equation of integral dependence of
f
over
\mathcal{A}
. Then

(\varpi^{m}f)^{n}+a_{1}\varpi^{m}(\varpi^{m}f)^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}\varpi^{m(n-1)}(\varpi^{m}f)+a_{n}\varpi^{mn}=0

is an equation of integral dependence over
\mathcal{A}
satisfied by
\varpi^{m}f
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\nu(\varpi^{m}f)\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|\varpi^{mi}a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}

\displaystyle \leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|\varpi^{mi}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}=|\varpi^{m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}.

Since this holds for every equation of integral dependence of
f
over
\mathcal{A}
, we conclude that
\nu(\varpi^{m}f)\leq|\varpi^{m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\nu(f)
for all
f\in\mathcal{B}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, as desired.

Now we verify, following the last part of the proof of
[
21
]
, Ch. II, Proposition 2, that
\nu
satisfies the second condition in Lemma
2.33
, i.e., we prove that
\nu(f)^{m}\leq\nu(f^{m})
for all
f\in\mathcal{B}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Given an equation of integral dependence

(f^{m})^{n}+a_{1}(f^{m})^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}f^{m}+a_{n}=0

of
f^{m}
over
\mathcal{A}
, we can rewrite this equation as

f^{mn}+b_{m}f^{mn-m}+\dots+b_{nm-m}f^{m}+b_{nm}=0

with
b_{m(n-i)}=a_{n-i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. We obtain:

\nu(f)\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|b_{m(n-i)}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/m(n-i)}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{n-i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/m(n-i)}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/mi}.

Thus

\nu(f)^{m}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i}.

Since this holds for every equation of integral dependence of
f^{m}
over
\mathcal{A}
, we get the desired inequality
\nu(f)^{m}\leq\nu(f^{m})
. This finally allows us to apply Lemma
2.33
to the function
\nu
and conclude that
\nu\leq p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
.

Therefore, to finish the proof of the equality
\nu=|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
it suffices to prove that
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}\leq|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. Note that, since
U
is an integrally closed subset of
\mathcal{A}
, we have
U^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}=U
, so that

p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}|_{\mathcal{A}}=p_{U,\varpi}=|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi},

where for the last equality we used Proposition
2.35
. By Proposition
2.31
and Proposition
2.36
, the function
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is submultiplicative and power-multiplicative, so, by Theorem
2.18
(
[
21
]
, Ch. I, Cor. du Théorème 1), we see that

p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}(f)=\max_{v\in{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})}v(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}
, where
(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
is the seminormed monoid obtained by equipping the underlying multiplicative monoid of
\mathcal{B}
with the submultiplicative function
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
and where
{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
is the constructible spectrum of
(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
(see Definition
2.16
). Thus it suffices to prove that every

v\in{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})

is bounded above by
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. Since we have seen
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
to be a ring seminorm, every element of
{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
is a multiplicative ring seminorm bounded above by
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
, by Proposition
2.17
. Therefore, by Lemma
8.15
and Lemma
2.10
, to prove that every element of
{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
is bounded above by
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
, it suffices to prove that the restriction to
\mathcal{A}
of any
v\in{\rm Min}(\mathcal{B},p_{U^{\prime},\varpi})
is bounded above by
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. However, this immediately follows from the observation that both
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
and
p_{U^{\prime},\varpi}
have the same restriction
p_{U,\varpi}=|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
to
\mathcal{A}
.∎

Recall from
[
3
]
, Proposition 5.15, that for any integral extension of domains
A\hookrightarrow B
with
A
a normal domain the coefficients of the minimal polynomial (over the field of fractions of
A
) of any
f\in B
lie in
A
.

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of domains with
A
normal. Let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero non-unit in both
A
and
B
such that
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
(since
A
is normal, it is automatically integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
). If
f\in\mathcal{B}
satisfies
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
, then the coefficients
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
of the minimal polynomial of
f
over the fraction field of
A
satisfy
|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

We follow the first paragraphs of the proof of
[
21
]
, Ch. II, Proposition 3. If
f\in\mathcal{B}
satisfies
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}<1
, then by Proposition
8.18
there exists an equation of integral dependence

f^{m}+b_{1}f^{m-1}+\dots+b_{m-1}f+b_{m}=0

with
b_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
|b_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}<1
for all
i
. In particular,
f
belongs to the integral closure
\mathcal{B}^{+}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
inside
\mathcal{B}
, so the coefficients of the minimal polynomial of
f
over the field of fractions of
\mathcal{A}
belong to the normal domain
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
.

It remains to prove the assertion for
f\in\mathcal{B}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
. Suppose that there exists an index
i_{0}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
with
|a_{i_{0}}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}>1
. First assume that there exists an element
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\alpha
is multiplicative with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
and

1>|\alpha|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}>|a_{i_{0}}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{-1/i_{0}}.

Then, on one hand, we have
|\alpha f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=|\alpha|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}<1
and, on the other hand,
\alpha^{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}
is a coefficient of the minimal polynomial of
\alpha f
with
|\alpha^{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}>1
, which contradicts what we proved in the last paragraph.

In the general case, we can adjoin an element
\alpha
with the above properties by the following procedure. For any real number
r>0
we can define a seminormed
\mathcal{A}
-algebra

\mathcal{A}[r^{-1}X]=(\mathcal{A}[X],|\cdot|_{r})

by letting
|\cdot|_{r}
be the
r
-Gauss norm

|\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{j}X^{j}|_{r}=\max_{0\leq j\leq n}|c_{j}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}r^{j},

and similarly for
(\mathcal{B},|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi})
. By construction, the maps
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}[r^{-1}X]
and
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}[r^{-1}X]
are isometries (when their sources are endowed with their respective spectral seminorms) and
X
is a seminorm-multiplicative element in
\mathcal{A}[r^{-1}X]
and in
\mathcal{B}[r^{-1}X]
with seminorm
|X|_{r}=r
. Since
A
is a normal domain, so is
A[X]
. Since
B
is a domain integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
, the polynomial ring
B[X]
is also a domain integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}[X]
. Moreover,
A[X]\hookrightarrow B[X]
is an integral extension and for
f\in\mathcal{B}
the minimal polynomial of
f
over the field of fractions
{\rm Frac}(A)
of
A
is equal to the minimal polynomial of
f
, viewed as an element of
\mathcal{B}[X]
, over the field of fractions of
A[X]
. It follows that, to prove the inequality
|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
for the coefficients of the minimal polynomial of some
f\in\mathcal{B}
, it suffices to prove the analogous assertion for the minimal polynomial of
f
regarded as an element of
\mathcal{B}[r^{-1}X]
for some choice of
r>0
. Choosing
r
with
1>r>|a_{i_{0}}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{-1/i_{0}}
, we can thus apply the argument in the preceding paragraph to conclude.∎

Proposition 8.20

(Analog of
[
21
]
, Ch. II. Proposition 3)
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of integral domains and suppose that
A
is a normal domain. Set
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero non-unit in both
A
and
B
and suppose that
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
. Then for every
f\in\mathcal{B}
the spectral seminorm of
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
can be described as

|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{1/i},

where
X^{n}+a_{1}X^{n-1}+\dots+a_{n-1}X+a_{n}
is the minimal polynomial of
f
over the fraction field of
A
.

Proof.

We continue following the proof of
[
21
]
, Ch. II, Proposition 3. Suppose first that
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=0
. Then
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq|\varpi^{m}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
for all
m\geq 1
. In other words,
|\varpi^{-m}f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
for all
m\geq 1
. For any
m\geq 1
, the coefficients of the minimal polynomial of
\varpi^{-m}f
are
\varpi^{-mi}a_{i}
, so
|\varpi^{-mi}a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
for all
i=1,\dots,n
and all
m\geq 1
, by Lemma
8.19
. It follows that
|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=0
for all
i=1,\dots,n
, which proves the proposition for elements
f\in\mathcal{B}
with
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=0
.

Now assume that
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\neq 0
. By the same procedure as in the last paragraph of the proof of Lemma
8.19
, we may assume that there exists an element
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
which is multiplicative with respect to the seminorm
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
and
|\alpha|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}^{-1}
. Under this assumption, the assertion of the proposition reduces to the special case when
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
. In that case, we know that the coefficients
a_{i}
of the minimal polynomial of
f
satisfy
|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}\leq 1
, by Lemma
8.19
. But if
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}<1
, then, by Prop.
8.18
,
|f|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}<1
, a contradiction. It follows that
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|a_{i}|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}=1
, as desired.∎

Proposition 8.21
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of integral domains with
A
normal and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero non-unit. If
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
and
A
is completely integrally in
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
, then
B
is actually completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Since
A
,
B
are integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, Lemma
4.26
tells us that the Tate rings
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are uniform. By
[
13
]
, Lemma 2.24, we then know that
A
(respectively,
B
) is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
(respectively, in
\mathcal{B}
) if and only if it is equal to the closed unit ball with respect to
|\cdot|_{{\rm sp},\varpi}
. We conclude by applying Lemma
8.19
or Proposition
8.20
. ∎

We can now prove our main theorem on (completed) integral extensions.

Theorem 8.22
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow B
be an integral extension of domains with
A
a normal domain and let
\varpi\in A
be a non-zero non-unit in
A
and in
B
. If
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov (respectively, strongly
\varpi
-Krull) and
B
is integrally closed in
B[\varpi^{-1}]
, then
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov (respectively, strongly
\varpi
-Krull). In particular, the
\varpi
-adic completion
\widehat{B}
of
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{A}=A[\varpi^{-1}]
,
\mathcal{B}=B[\varpi^{-1}]
. Suppose that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov and
B
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
. By Corollary
6.14
, the assumption that
A
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov implies that
A
is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{A}
. By Proposition
8.21
, this implies that
B
is completely integrally closed in
\mathcal{B}
. By Proposition
8.3
,
B
is weakly
\varpi
-Shilov. In view of Proposition
6.18
and Theorem
6.20
, to prove that
B
is strongly
\varpi
-Shilov, it only remains to prove that the set of pre-images under
{\rm sp}
of generic points of
{\rm Spf}(B)
is a boundary for
\mathcal{B}
. Let
\mathcal{S}={\rm sp}^{-1}({\rm Spf}(B)_{{\rm gen}})
and
f\in\mathcal{B}
